* El Niño Bakes Southeast Asia
  Foreign Policy | 05 Aug 05:00 UTC
  The world’s rice bowl takes a hit.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: This year’s El Niño might end up being the strongest in 140 years, Aung San Suu Kyi is confirmed alive by the Red Cross, and Malaysia takes an increasingly hard line on the Rohingya. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: This year’s El Niño might end up being the strongest in 140 years, Aung San Suu Kyi is confirmed alive by the Red Cross, and Malaysia takes an increasingly hard line on the Rohingya. This year’s El Niño is underway, and it might end up being the strongest in 140 years. Southeast Asia will be hit hard. The Asia-Pacific will be the worst-affected region, according to insurance giant Munich Re , with heat waves, drought, wildfires, and increased typhoon risk. The region is already facing higher prices as result of the Iran war, and El Niño will only intensify that. As I have previously flagged, high food prices could lead to political instability in the region. This year’s El Niño follows another strong El Niño event, which lasted from 2023 to 2024. Effects of that one, such as increased farmer debt , are still being felt in the region’s agricultural sector. The effects on Southeast Asia will also be felt around the world, as the region is a major producer of staple crops. Thailand and Vietnam are the world’s second and third-largest exporters of rice. Indonesia and Malaysia produce 85 percent of the world’s palm oil. Vietnam and Indonesia are the world’s second and fourth-largest producers of coffee. Rice and palm oil are particularly sensitive to climate anomalies. Experts have estimated that rice yields in the region could fall between 2 to 8 percent. El Niño is predicted to last up until spring 2027, which means that effects on food supply and prices will persist into 2028. The Philippines and Timor Leste are particularly high-risk countries, according to the World Food Program, with the main risk being droughts and floods affecting staple crops. Admittedly, these two countries are not agricultural powerhouses in the region. But Cambodia, Indonesia, Myanmar, Thailand, and Vietnam all face a serious drought risk, as well. Thailand received roughly 60 percent less rain than normal between January and April. The Bangkok export price for rice reached $490 per metric ton at the end of June, up 27.5 percent since the start of 2026. * China Launches New Crackdown on Organized Crime
  Foreign Policy | 04 Aug 21:06 UTC
  From local gangs to sand mafias, Beijing keeps running into the same problems.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: Beijing launches a new crackdown on organized crime ; a slate of racist, AI-generated videos floods the Chinese internet; and China plans to conduct an asteroid defense test . Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: Beijing launches a new crackdown on organized crime ; a slate of racist, AI-generated videos floods the Chinese internet; and China plans to conduct an asteroid defense test . China has launched a one-year “special campaign” against organized crime , promising a crackdown on enterprises ranging from online scam syndicates to human trafficking. These campaigns are a semiregular feature of Chinese politics. The last major one, named the “Special Campaign to Sweep Away Black Societies and Eliminate Evil Forces,” ran from 2018 to 2021. An official Q&A notes a focus on six types of “bullies”—a term used in previous government crackdowns to describe gangs that dominate what should be public spaces or state resources. (“Bullies” is the state media translation. The Chinese word is ba , which is akin to a local tyrant; “thugs” or “racketeers” might be closer in English.) This list includes “village bullies” and “township bullies,” or local gangs that effectively control small communities, as well as “street bullies” and “city bullies,” which run protection rackets targeting street vendors and small businesses. But it’s the final pairing—“mining bullies” and “sand bullies”—that stands out. The first of these is unsurprising. Mining has long been a major nexus of organized crime in China, with criminal groups building mines within state-run operations, digging illegal tunnels, and employing off-the-books migrant labor. China has cracked down on illegal rare-earth mining since 2009 , shrinking an illicit market that was once roughly half the size of the legal one. That success helps explain why China’s restrictions on exports of critical minerals to the United States last year proved to be far more effective than similar restrictions imposed on Japan in 2010; this time, the ban was not undercut by smuggling. “Sand bullies,” however, require a bit of explanation. The term is rather literal. High-quality sand, mostly from rivers and lakes, is an essential ingredient in concrete. Illegal sand mining is an enormous business worldwide, estimated to be worth between $200 billion and $350 billion annually. In 2021 alone, China’s sand and gravel demand was approximately
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/08/04/china-crime-illegal-mining-sand-industry-crackdown/

* Will Trump Destroy the Deep Oceans?
  Foreign Policy | 04 Aug 19:49 UTC
  Seabed mining is a threat to both ecology and the international order.
  FULL TEXT: There has been so much turbulence above water in the Strait of Hormuz and now the Red Sea that you’ll be forgiven for missing big news about what is going on under the waves. The United States has created a new Marine Minerals Administration, which will administer the mining of minerals from the seabed in domestic waters. But American firms, with the government’s blessing, are also speeding ahead with plans for mining in international waters, even though the U.N. Convention on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS) bans such unilateral mining. The seabed is home to tantalizingly vast quantities of minerals. They reside in potato-sized polymetallic nodules, which contain nickel, copper, manganese, cobalt, and, most alluringly, rare earths. Explorers and entrepreneurs have dreamt about mining them since seabed exploration began more than a century ago. Their desire is understandable because the nodules are plentiful. The Clarion-Clipperton Zone (CCZ), a vast expanse between Hawaii and Mexico, is home to an estimated 21.1 billion dry tons of the nodules—more than any other part of the world. * India’s Political Atmosphere Has Changed
  Foreign Policy | 04 Aug 15:56 UTC
  If Modi responds to the Cockroach protesters with further repression, it could backfire.
  FULL TEXT: When the writer V.S. Naipaul traveled across India decades ago, he famously described the country as a land of a million mutinies, fracturing into myriad localized particularities, each subcaste and interest group with its own sectarian voice. Recent events in India, however, show how a splintered society can start to coalesce. The fierce, Generation Z-led revolt that spilled over last month was directed squarely at Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government. The movement, which initially christened itself the Cockroach Janta Party in a satirical response to a derogatory remark by India’s chief justice, picked up young people’s anger over leaked exam papers and brought tens of thousands of protesters to the streets of New Delhi to march toward Parliament. When the writer V.S. Naipaul traveled across India decades ago, he famously described the country as a land of a million mutinies, fracturing into myriad localized particularities, each subcaste and interest group with its own sectarian voice. Recent events in India, however, show how a splintered society can start to coalesce. The fierce, Generation Z-led revolt that spilled over last month was directed squarely at Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government. The movement, which initially christened itself the Cockroach Janta Party in a satirical response to a derogatory remark by India’s chief justice, picked up young people’s anger over leaked exam papers and brought tens of thousands of protesters to the streets of New Delhi to march toward Parliament. On July 25, they won a stunning victory with the resignation of Education Minister Dharmendra Pradhan. These young people turned anger into action and forced a powerful leader to listen, discarding the traditional Indian deference to authority or age—with smartphones in hand, even as police deployed batons and pellet guns. As with recent youth-led movements in Bangladesh, Nepal, and Sri Lanka, violent police action in India failed to deter the protesters. The movement exposed a new form of political pressure that was faster, looser, and harder to contain, underscoring how politics now works for a generation that has grown up online. The future of Indian democracy depends on the response of the state in the coming weeks and months. If Modi responds to the protests as a security challenge instead of a warning about public sentiment, he risks deepening the grievances that sparked the unrest and transforming them into a protracted crisis.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/08/04/india-cockroach-protests-modi-politics-change/

He pledged to recover stolen assets, strengthen law enforcement, and take down those he deems to be part of the corrupt elite , regardless of status or political standing. Makarim’s case is the highest profile in a string of investigations under President Prabowo Subianto, who made tackling corruption a centerpiece of his populist election campaign and his presidency since 2024. He pledged to recover stolen assets, strengthen law enforcement, and take down those he deems to be part of the corrupt elite , regardless of status or political standing. Other high-profile investigations during Prabowo’s term have involved politically connected businessmen, state-owned enterprises, and former ministers. In July, Indonesia’s top anti-corruption prosecutor Febrie Adriansyah resigned from his role in the attorney general’s office after police raids uncovered a stash of gold bars and almost $20 million in cash in a residence that he owns. Adriansyah is now facing allegations of corruption and money laundering connected to several state-owned enterprise cases. (He has denied any wrongdoing.) Prabowo’s supporters argue that these investigations demonstrate a willingness to pursue corruption at the highest levels, even when it implicates popular public figures such as Makarim. Critics say they instead reflect selective enforcement and political maneuvering, rather than genuine institutional reform. While many investigations may be entirely legitimate, a closer look at who is being targeted, the institutional rivalries behind certain cases, and the government’s limited appetite for deeper reform raises questions about whether Indonesia is building a stronger anti-corruption system or simply reshuffling power among the country’s political and economic elite. * America’s Cosmic Bet on AI
  Foreign Policy | 04 Aug 13:50 UTC
  The winner takes it all. What happens if the United States loses?
  FULL TEXT: The United States is betting the house on winning the artificial intelligence race with China. Consider the U.S. economy, which is increasingly becoming a highly leveraged bet on the promise of this new technology. AI is the primary driver of investments, stock market gains, and growth in the United States. As The Wall Street Journal recently noted , AI-related investment is driving most of the growth in U.S. GDP. Spending on AI data centers accounts for half of all business investment in the country. AI companies made up more than 40 percent of the value of U.S. equity markets before SpaceX went public in June .   FULL TEXT: NORTHEASTERN SYRIA—At a former U.S. military base outside of the Syrian city of Hasakeh, Samir Asso—better known by his nom de guerre, Sipan Hamo—sat down for a conversation with Foreign Policy in early July. Now Hamo is Syria’s deputy defense minister for the eastern region, the first Kurdish commander to hold a senior military post in Syrian President Ahmed al-Sharaa’s government. The room where we spoke featured a large desk, which Hamo sat casually in front of, and several signs bearing the green eagle emblem of the Syrian regime. Hamo’s new role reflects the latest stage in an ongoing confrontation between Damascus and the Kurdish-led autonomous administration that ruled northeast Syria for the past decade. After 10 months of stalled talks, during which Washington’s support decisively shifted from the Kurds to the Syrian government, clashes erupted in January. Within 24 hours , Syrian forces defeated the SDF and captured 80 percent of Kurdish-held territory. A broader agreement at the end of January laid out a phased plan to incorporate the region’s security, military, and administrative institutions into the Syrian state. But as the last six months show, folding the Kurds’ vision of autonomous, left-wing rule under the authority of a predominantly Arab, Islamist-dominated regime remains a fraught, rocky process. * The Architecture of Israel’s Genocide in Gaza
  Foreign Policy | 04 Aug 10:00 UTC
  A new book describes the destruction of Palestinian life street by street.
  FULL TEXT: While many Americans believe that Israel has committed a genocide against Palestinians in Gaza over the past three years, attempts to understand what really happened there and hold Israel accountable have fallen short so far. The legal case at the International Court of Justice has been stalled by American and European opposition. The ferocious debate over the term itself has degenerated into partisan flamethrowing and definitional nitpicking. The campus protests that erupted in the spring of 2024 were violently repressed and have largely gone away. A new book by the British Israeli architect and political activist Eyal Weizman tries to fill that void by telling the story of Israel’s systematic destruction of Gaza street by street, farm by farm, and hospital by hospital. What sets Ungrounding: The Architecture of Genocide apart from this polarized discourse is its determined focus on the physical and its analytical tone. The evidence, collected by Weizman and his team from audios, videos, and interviews and subjected to rigorous analysis, is overwhelming. Their efforts produced the “Cartography of Genocide,” an interactive and constantly evolving digital map that details “an organized and designed campaign to destroy Palestinian life in Gaza.” By the end of the book, the question is less whether Israel’s actions should be regarded as genocide and more what to make of a genocidal process that seeks to not only remove a people but to fundamentally and permanently transform the land.   FULL TEXT: The U.S. trade deficit in goods and services fell slightly to $73.3 billion in June as America imported fewer foreign computers and pharmaceuticals in the month. U.S. exports also fell slightly from a busy month in May , according to data the Commerce Department released on Tuesday. Exports dropped 0.9 percent in the month, to $314.7 billion, as petroleum exports fell back from a historical high the prior month. The combination decreased the monthly trade deficit, the gap between what the United States imports and what it exports. The U.S. trade deficit in goods and services fell 5.6 percent from the prior month. But both exports and imports of services hit record levels in June. Diane Swonk, chief economist at KPMG U.S., said that services exports had picked up in part because of increased tourism to the United States — what she called a “World Cup effect.” “That’s considered an export, along with their purchases of ranch dressing ,” she joked. Ms. Swonk said that imports had actually been relatively strong in June, as companies tried to make foreign purchases ahead of a new round of tariffs. The scale of the yen purchases was not disclosed. During a cabinet meeting on Friday, a photograph of Mr. Bessent’s note pad — which he has previously said he keeps visible for the media to peruse — showed purchases of $5 billion to $10 billion worth of yen on his “to do” list. The ICC has always been controversial. Every prosecutor has left behind a complicated legacy. The court’s first prosecutor, Luis Moreno Ocampo, was accused of focusing too heavily on Africa, leading many to believe that the ICC was only targeting leaders from the continent. His successor, Fatou Bensouda, spent much of her term in office trying to move beyond that criticism while facing resistance over investigations involving Afghanistan and Palestine. Karim Khan expanded the court’s reach with some of its most consequential cases, including the arrest warrant issued for Putin and the case against Netanyahu. But his departure has created another crisis for an institution already struggling with questions about its credibility. The next prosecutor will inherit a court under unprecedented political pressure. The United States and Israel have intensified efforts to undermine the institution, while some member states have questioned whether the court can continue to function without stronger political support. At the same time, millions of victims of atrocities around the world continue to see the ICC as a necessary check on governments that fail to hold their own leaders accountable. The choice of chief prosecutor will be important, but it will not resolve the deeper questions facing the institution, which revolve around whether the ICC can maintain its independence while operating in a world shaped by power politics. For some observers, however, the attacks against the ICC are not evidence of failure. * A Haitian American Reflects on the Future
  New Lines | 04 Aug 11:02 UTC
  The Trump administration is ending the program that allows many Haitians to live and work in the U.S. A Haitian American reflects on the damage the move will do to communities, the economy and the country’s ethos — and the underlying hostility that appears to have guided Trump’s decision. The post A Haitian American Reflects on the Future...
  FULL TEXT: Jan. 13, 2010, is a day that I will never forget. That Wednesday morning, I had several missed calls, text messages and notifications on my phone. I did not know it at the time, but the day before, a massive earthquake had struck near the capital city of Haiti, Port-au-Prince. As the child of Haitian immigrants who came to the United States in 1980, I have always kept up with events in Haiti, and this was, without question, the most devastating natural disaster that had occurred on the island in my lifetime. Hundreds of thousands of Haitians died as a result of the magnitude 7 earthquake, and millions more were severely impacted. At that point, I didn’t know all this — I was concerned for my family. Many of my cousins, uncles and aunts were still in Haiti. Were they okay? What would they do now that the capital had been damaged so severely? Where would they find refuge? On Jan. 15, in the midst of the chaos and confusion, the United States government extended temporary protected status (TPS) to Haitian nationals, giving them the ability to live and work in the United States without fear of deportation. Now, after 15 years of Haitians building their lives, integrating into their local communities and contributing to the economic and social fabric of the United States, the second Trump administration has abruptly ended TPS for Haiti and has plunged communities across the nation into a state of fear, anxiety and despair. Our industries, our hospitals, our nursing homes, our businesses and the entire U.S. economy will be harmed by this decision. TPS was established with the passage of the Immigration Act of 1990. The law significantly reshaped our immigration system and provided additional pathways for immigrants to come to the U.S. It raised the number of immigrants allowed to enter each year from 290,000 to 675,000 and introduced various programs, such as the Diversity Visa lottery and the H-1B Skilled Worker Visa Program, to increase the number of diverse, skilled workers entering the country. Along with these changes to the U.S. immigration system, this law established the TPS program, which, in this instance, provided work permits and protection for Haitians living in the U.S. after the earthquake. TPS designations are applied to countries that are experiencing extremely challenging circumstances, such as “on-going armed conflict, an environmental disaster, or extraordinary and temporary conditions,” as stated by the U.S. Department of Homeland Security.

* Magical Mystery Toads
  Longreads | 04 Aug 10:00 UTC
  The long, tangled, and mostly wrong history of the psychedelic toad.
  FULL TEXT: Kimon de Greef | Longreads | August 4, 2026 | 4,741 words (17 minutes) From The Ego Trip: Psychedelic Toads, a Trail of Deaths, and the Guru Who Peddled Transcendence by Kimon de Greef. Copyright © 2026 by Kimon de Greef. Excerpted by permission of Doubleday, an imprint of The Knopf Doubleday Group, a division of Penguin Random House LLC. All rights reserved. In 1935, two chemists at Tokyo’s Imperial University synthesized a new molecule and unwittingly launched one of the strangest narratives of the psychedelic age. Toshio Hoshino and Kenya Shimodaira were working on a family of compounds known as the tryptamines, which occur widely in nature and have a range of intriguing biological properties. The neurotransmitter serotonin, which among other things regulates mood, cognition, and social behavior, is a tryptamine. So are many psychoactive drugs, although this would only be discovered later on. The Japanese scientists had recently completed the world’s first laboratory synthesis of a molecule called bufotenine, which is produced by toads and certain species of mushrooms. Now, via a sequence of reactions, they engineered a close chemical relative. Diagrams of the two tryptamines looked nearly identical, each represented by two angular rings and a tail, resembling microscopic organisms; the only difference lay in the shape of the head. Such tiny changes in chemical structure “can lead to radically different consequences, like a railroad’s switch points,” as the writer and organic chemist Primo Levi once wrote. The new molecule’s name, following scientific convention, would be 5-MeO-DMT. Hoshino and Shimodaira published their findings in the Bulletin of the Chemical Society of Japan, leaving no explanation as to why they had conducted these experiments. The early 20th century was a time of wide-ranging exploration in organic chemistry, with researchers synthesizing new compounds to assess their pharmacological potential or better understand the reactions by which one substance could be transformed into another. Many of these compounds had no evident utility, such as 5-MeO-DMT, which for decades was thought only to be a product of the laboratory. This changed in 1959, when the molecule was isolated from a shrub native to Brazil. Not long afterward, 5-MeO-DMT was detected in Anadenanthera peregrina, a leguminous tree from the Caribbean and South America. For thousands of years, Indigenous communities had been harvesting its slender bean pods to prepare snuffs

* What the Ceuta Crisis Reveals About the New Mediterranean Order
  New Lines | 03 Aug 16:46 UTC
  The Spanish-administered North African enclave has long been an anomaly, but recent crossings have put it at the center of overlapping struggles — over migration, Gaza, Western Sahara and the future of Europe. The post What the Ceuta Crisis Reveals About the New Mediterranean Order appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Since tens of thousands of people crossed into Ceuta last week, the political battle over the small, Spanish-administered North African enclave has eclipsed the crossings themselves. Within hours, right-wing and anti-immigration factions were presenting Ceuta as proof of Europe’s border collapse. Israeli ministers used the episode to attack Spain over its increasingly confrontational stance toward both Israel and the Trump administration. Underneath it all were the migrants themselves, at least 72 of whom died. Since the Abraham Accords, Morocco has become one of Israel’s closest Arab security partners while receiving unprecedented American support for its claim over the Western Sahara . Just two weeks ago, the House Appropriations Committee recommended at least $40 million in assistance for Morocco while describing both Ceuta and Melilla as “located in Moroccan territory.” Last week, the U.S. president claimed that Morocco had named part of the Tiznit-Dakhla highway through Western Sahara after him, a claim later reinforced by a letter from the king.   FULL TEXT: Hosted by Faisal Al Yafai Featuring Ryan Gingeras Produced by Finbar Anderson Listen to and follow The Lede Apple Podcasts | Spotify | Youtube | Podbean Historian Ryan Gingeras relishes an opportunity to discuss culture — especially when it intersects with the topic of his new book, “Mafia: A Global History.” In “The Godfather Part II,” Gingeras tells Faisal Al Yafai on The Lede, we see how “the American mafia is not only a critical element of the country’s history, it’s reflective of some of the more formative elements in the making of the modern U.S.” As demonstrated by his new book’s subtitle, while movies like “The Godfather” focus on criminal networks in the United States, such groups exist globally. Referencing one of the principal antagonists in the first “Godfather” film, he says, “The [Virgil] Sollozzo character in real life more likely would have had second, perhaps even thirdhand connections to the opium fields. The people who brought the refined morphine could have been Armenian, they could have been Lebanese, most likely Lebanese Christians. And the people who actually brought him the actual heroin, the dope, most likely would’ve been French.” Gingeras and Al Yafai’s discussion begins with American mafia culture, but expands both geographically and thematically, taking in how networks like those depicted on screen helped to build and shape the modern nations in which they now operate. Gingeras himself, an expert in Ottoman history, came to realize the personal impact of such networks when travelling to Turkey for work. “It took me leaving not so much New York, but leaving the United States and coming to Turkey to figure out that this was something that was a bigger story,” Gingeras says. “People all over the world struggled with this idea of, ‘What does the world mean to me when mafias are such an important part of it?’ In New York, it’s second nature, but what we, especially in the United States, oftentimes fail to really grasp is that this is something people struggle with everywhere.” Despite the fact they operate in the underworld, mafias matter to people everywhere, Gingeras argues, because they bring into relief the blurred edges of the nation-state. “Mafias are people with faces,” Gingeras says.

Others warn of the security risks posed by the powerful, free-to-download models. Whether to embrace or reject these Chinese models has become a divisive issue in the U.S. tech industry. Chinese open models are cost-effective. Open-weight models are free to download, but running them incurs computing costs. Many users pay to access the models through their developers or third-party cloud providers. Facing ballooning AI costs, some U.S. companies and developers have been diverting simpler tasks to Chinese models to save money. Companies can also use Chinese open-weight models to build customized AI systems, or host the models locally so they don’t have to share data with external parties. Chinese open models benefit AI infrastructure providers and companies that need affordable AI services. Nvidia has been a leading voice in support of open-weight models. The availability of open models creates a bigger customer base for its AI chips. On July 24, Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang posted on X for the first time to share an open letter stressing the importance of open models in expanding access to the AI economy. Other tech giants, including Microsoft, Google, and Meta, have since joined the letter.   As the war nears the end of its third year, many in Gaza are marrying without waiting for peace, reshaping traditions of celebration and mourning as they struggle to hold on to life. The post How War Has Redefined Marriage in Gaza appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: When I arrived at the wedding, the bride was not the first thing that caught my eye. It was the portrait of a young man killed in the war, hanging behind the spot where the bride and groom would stand just minutes later. No one had taken it down or tried to conceal it behind the white fabric draped as a simple backdrop for the ceremony. It remained in place, framed by a few artificial flowers, as guests settled into plastic chairs and children darted between the tents nearby. In Gaza, joy no longer comes after mourning has ended. The two now exist side by side. A few minutes later, the bride entered to the sound of ululations. There was no long procession of cars, no brightly lit wedding hall like those that once hosted celebrations across Gaza. Instead, the family had carved out a small space inside a displacement camp, trying, if only for a few hours, to make it feel different from the rows of tents surrounding it. Halfway through the music, the speakers suddenly fell silent. It was not a power cut — Gaza has had virtually no electricity since the war began — but a drained battery powering the sound system. The celebration paused. For a brief moment, several guests instinctively looked up as the familiar buzz of an Israeli surveillance drone passed overhead. A few minutes later, one of the young men returned with another battery. The music resumed, and so did the ululations, as though these interruptions had become just another part of a Gaza wedding, no less familiar than the constant sound of drones. Watching the scene unfold, I realized this wedding was unlike any I had known before the war. It was not only the setting that had changed. The meaning of celebration itself had been transformed. Before the war, weddings in Gaza were planned months in advance. Families booked lavish wedding halls, prepared long guest lists and celebrated late into the night. If a close relative died, weddings were almost always postponed for months — sometimes for an entire year — in keeping with long-standing traditions of mourning passed down through generations. Today, after nearly three years of war, those customs belong to a life that can no longer be afforded.
